description: "How the ZX Spectrum Next's four 8-bit DACs work: a level the CPU writes and the DAC holds, four channels on two sides, the ports of six Spectrum and Pentagon sound cards and the NextReg $84 bits that enable them, the ports they share, the NextReg mirrors, four ways to feed samples on time (a CPU loop, the CTC, the DMA and the Copper), a drum hit, how much of the mixer the DACs take, and how Klive emulates them."
---

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# The 8-Bit DACs: Four Channels, Six Sound Cards

An AY chip can whistle, buzz and hiss, and with a little help it can even play a decent bass. But ask it to
say "Game over" in a human voice, or to play a real snare drum, and it shrugs. It only knows square waves,
noise and envelopes. A **DAC** knows nothing at all, and that is exactly its strength: it plays whatever
number you give it. Give it the right numbers fast enough, and out comes a voice, a drum kit or a whole song
from a file. The Next has four of them, and it answers on the ports of six different Spectrum and Pentagon
sound cards, so the software written for those cards plays without a single change. How does one machine
pretend to be six cards at once?

Sampled sound is what lifts a game above the bleeps. A drum hit on a DAC over AY music makes a chip tune
sound like a band. A spoken word at the start of a level, a sampled explosion or a scream of guitar is
something no tone generator can fake. And for a whole family of music players, the ones that play tracker
modules with recorded instruments, a DAC is the only way in.

Read [The Big Picture: Four Sources, One Mixer](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/overview/)
first: it introduces the DACs and the difference between a generator, which plays on its own, and a level
writer, which needs the CPU at every step. A DAC is the purest level writer there is. If samples and DACs are
new to you, the background chapter
[How a Computer Makes Sound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/) explains them, and I will
build on it rather than repeat it.

## The road ahead

We begin with what one DAC channel does, which takes about a minute, because it does almost nothing: it holds
a number. Then the four channels meet the two sides of the mixer, and the switch that turns them on. Next comes
the fun part, the port map: which port of which sound card reaches which channel, the bits that turn each card
on and off, and the ports two cards would both like to own. After a quick look at the NextReg mirrors, we
arrive at the question that decides whether a sample sounds right or wrong, and the one I would ask you to
remember if you remember only one: who times the writes? A CPU loop, the CTC, the DMA and the Copper each do
it differently, and only some of them keep their pitch when the CPU changes speed. We end with a drum hit, the
DACs' share of the mixer, and how Klive does the same.

<Callout type="info">
The numbers are for the Next's **28 MHz master clock**, with the CPU at **3.5 MHz** unless a capture says
otherwise. Levels are a DAC channel's own **8-bit value**, `$00` to `$FF`, or the **units of the Next's
mixer**, where a side's two channels count four times. The waveforms are captured from Klive's emulated Next
at 48,000 samples a second, from programs checked to do what the text says; each shows one channel's level as
Klive's mix carries it.
</Callout>

## A number that holds

A DAC channel is an **8-bit register** with a converter behind it. Your program writes a value, `$00` to
`$FF`, and the channel turns it into a level. Then it does nothing at all until the next write. No counter, no
clock, no envelope: the level simply **holds**. Write a new number and the level jumps to it; write the same
number again and nothing changes.

So a DAC makes a waveform only if the program writes one step of it after another, on time. Here is a channel
written once every millisecond, with eight values that go up, down and round again:

<figure>
{/* fig:stair */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 246" role="img" aria-label="Ten milliseconds of one DAC channel's level, from $00 to $FF, with a dashed line at the rest level $80. The level is a staircase: it jumps to each value the program writes, $80, $C0, $FF, $C0, $80, $40, $00, $40 and round again, and stays flat for a millisecond until the next write. Small ticks under the plot mark the moments the level changed." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="0" y="91" fill="#30a46c" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">port $DF</text>
  <text x="0" y="110" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">one write</text>

</svg>
{/* /fig:stair */}
<figcaption>One DAC channel written through port <code>$DF</code> once every 3,500 T-states, a millisecond at 3.5 MHz. Each value holds until the next write, so the level is a staircase. The ticks under the plot mark the writes.</figcaption>
</figure>

See the dashed line in the middle? That is `$80`, the **rest level**. A DAC's values run from 0 upwards, and
a sound has to swing both ways, so silence is the middle of the range: a sample that goes up and down by the
same amount swings about `$80`. Every channel starts there after a reset. A sample that ends at `$80` leaves the
channel silent; one that ends anywhere else leaves a small constant level, which you will not hear (a capacitor
after the converters removes any constant level, as the mixer chapter explains), but which takes some of the
room the other sources could use.

## Four channels, two sides

The Next has four channels, **A, B, C and D**. A and B belong to the left side, C and D to the right:

</svg>
{/* /fig:channels */}
<figcaption>The four DAC channels. Each side adds its two channels, 0 to 510, and the mixer counts that sum four times: up to 2,040 units a side.</figcaption>
</figure>

Each side adds its two channels into a 9-bit number, 0 to 510, and the mixer multiplies it by 4, so the DACs
can give a side up to **2,040 units**. Why two channels a side, when one would do? Because that is what it takes to
copy every card: Soundrive drives four channels, two a side, Covox one a side, and the mono cards a single
channel that you hear on both. Four channels cover them all, as you will see in a moment.

The DACs have a switch of their own: bit 3 of the **Peripheral 3 Setting register, NextReg `$08`**. It is 0
after power-on, so the DACs start **off**. While it is 0, every channel is held at `$80`, and every write to
them, through a port or a NextReg, is ignored. Set the bit, and the channels start at `$80` and follow your
writes. Clear it again, and they go back to `$80` at once: a quick way to silence all four.

## Six sound cards on four channels

Back in the day, a Spectrum or Pentagon owner who wanted sampled sound plugged in a sound card built around one or
more DACs, and every card picked its own ports. Software followed the cards, so a program written for one card
writes to that card's ports and nowhere else. The Next answers on **all of them**. Here is the whole map: each
card, the bit that enables it, and the port that writes each channel:

<figcaption>Every DAC port of the Next. A card's ports answer only while its bit of NextReg <code>$84</code> is 1; the NextRegs need no enable. A cell with the same port in two columns is a mono port: one write reaches both channels.</figcaption>
</figure>

A few things in the map deserve a closer look.

**A port is its low byte.** The DACs look only at the low 8 bits of the port address, so `$00DF`, `$12DF` and
`$7FDF` all reach Specdrum's channels. That keeps `out ($df),a` working whatever is in A, which is exactly what
an `out (n),a` instruction puts in the high byte.

**Mono ports write two channels.** Pentagon's `$FB` and Specdrum's `$DF` write A and D, GS Covox's `$B3` writes
B and C. One channel on each side, both set to the same value: the sample plays in the middle, at the same
level as a stereo card playing it on both channels.

**The enables.** Each card's ports answer only while its bit is set in NextReg `$84`, one byte of the four
**Internal Port Decoding Enables registers, NextRegs `$82`–`$85`**. Bits 1 to 7 of NextReg `$84` belong to the
DAC cards, in the order of the map (bit 0 is the AY chips' ports). After power-on every bit is 1, so every card
answers. A program that needs one of those ports for something else clears the bit, and the DAC lets go of it.

**The ports two cards share.** Some cards picked the same ports, and the Next sorts them out like this:

- `$0F` and `$4F` belong to both Soundrive mode 1 and Covox, and `$5F` to both Soundrive mode 1 and Profi Covox.
  They always reach the same channel, so two cards enabled at once simply agree.
- `$FB` is the one real clash. Soundrive mode 2 uses it for channel D alone; Pentagon uses it for A and D at
  once. **Soundrive mode 2 wins**: Pentagon's `$FB` answers only while bit 2 is clear, and then a write to `$FB`
  reaches both A and D.
- `$F1` and `$F9`, two of Soundrive mode 2's ports, end in the same two address bits as `$FD`, and the 128K's
  paging ports `$7FFD`, `$DFFD` and `$1FFD` look at only a few address bits. Without care, a sample written to
  `$7FF1` would also page your memory around. Hey, that would make for an exciting crash. So while Soundrive
  mode 2 is on, a write to `$F1` or `$F9` reaches the DAC only.
- `$1F` is Soundrive mode 1's channel A for writes and the Kempston joystick for reads, and `$DF` is Specdrum's
  port for writes and, while Specdrum's bit is set and the mouse's ports are disabled, a second address of the
  joystick for reads. A write and a
  read never meet, so the DAC and the joystick share the ports in peace.

Let's hear the map at work. A Covox card has one channel per side: `$0F` writes B, on the left, and `$4F` writes
C, on the right. This loop writes a 16-step sine to the left and a 32-step one, an octave lower, to the right:

<figcaption>Covox stereo, both sides captured over the same five milliseconds. Port <code>$0F</code> plays on the left only, port <code>$4F</code> on the right only: 1,078 Hz and 539 Hz, from a step every 203 T-states.</figcaption>

## The NextReg mirrors

The ports are not the only way in. Three NextRegs write the DACs too: **NextReg `$2C`** writes channel B,
**NextReg `$2D`** writes A and D, and **NextReg `$2E`** writes C. They are there for code that has no free
port, and for the one device that can write NextRegs but no port at all: the **Copper**, as you will see shortly.
Their writes need no NextReg `$84` bit, but they do need the DACs to be on.

Reading the same three NextRegs gives you something else entirely: the samples arriving from the Raspberry Pi,
which belong to [the Pi chapter](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/pi-audio/#reading-the-pis-samples).

## Who times the writes?

Here comes the part that decides whether a sample sounds right. A DAC plays a sample at the rate your writes
arrive, and only at that rate. Write the steps of a recording made at 16,000 samples a second 16,000 times a
second, and it sounds as it was recorded. Write them faster and the voice turns into a chipmunk; write them at
an uneven pace and the sound gets a rough, gritty edge. So the real question about a DAC is not how to write it
(any `out` will do) but **who decides when**. The Next gives you four answers:

<figure>
{/* fig:timers */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 360" role="img" aria-label="A diagram of four ways to time DAC writes. The CPU clock, 3.5, 7, 14 or 28 MHz as NextReg $07 sets it, drives a CPU loop: its rate follows the CPU's speed. The 28 MHz master clock drives the other three: the CTC, which divides it by 16 or 256 and then by its time constant and interrupts the CPU, whose interrupt routine writes the sample; the DMA, which moves one byte every 32 times its prescaler master clocks; and the Copper, which can write a DAC through its NextReg mirror once per line. Those three keep their rate at every CPU speed." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>Four ways to time DAC writes. A CPU loop runs on the CPU's clock; the CTC, the DMA and the Copper count the 28 MHz master clock, so they keep their rate when the CPU changes speed.</figcaption>
</figure>

Let me walk you through them with the same 16-step sine, played each way.

**A CPU loop.** The simplest way of all: a loop that writes a step, waits, and
goes round again. This one writes a step every 218 T-states:

```z80klive
        .org $8000
Start:  di
        nextreg $07,0     ; 3.5 MHz
        nextreg $08,$58   ; the DACs on (bit 3)
        ld hl,Table
Loop:   ld a,(hl)         ; 7
        out ($df),a       ; 11: Specdrum, channels A and D
        ld a,l            ; 4
        inc a             ; 4
        and $0f           ; 7: the next of 16 steps
        ld l,a            ; 4
        ld b,13           ; 7
Dly:    djnz Dly          ; 13 * 12 + 8
        jp Loop           ; 10: 218 T-states a step
        .org $9000
Table:  .defb 128,177,218,245,255,245,218,177,128,79,38,11,1,11,38,79
```

At 3.5 MHz that is 3,500,000 ÷ 218 = 16,055 steps a second, and 16,055 ÷ 16 = **1,003 Hz**. The catch is that
the loop's rate is the CPU's rate. Switch the CPU to 7 MHz with the **CPU Speed register, NextReg `$07`**, and
the same loop plays twice as fast, an octave higher. Run it from contended memory at 3.5 MHz and it slows down
while the screen is drawn, as the beeper chapter showed. And while the loop plays, your program does nothing
else: the CPU is the sound card.

**The CTC.** The Next's CTC has four channels that count the 28 MHz master clock, divided first by a prescaler
of 16 or 256 and then by a **time constant** of 1 to 256. At every zero count it can interrupt the CPU, and the
interrupt routine writes the next step:

```z80klive
        .org $8000
Start:  di
        nextreg $07,0
        nextreg $08,$58
        ld a,$be
        ld i,a
        im 2
        nextreg $22,$04          ; ULA and line interrupts off
        nextreg $c0,$a1          ; hardware IM 2, vectors from $A0
        nextreg $c9,$ff          ; clear the CTC status
        ld bc,$183b
        ld a,$85                 ; interrupt, timer, prescaler 16, time constant follows, control word
        out (c),a
        ld a,109
        out (c),a
        ei
Park:   jr Park
Isr:    push af
        push hl
        ld hl,(Ptr)
        ld a,(hl)
        out ($df),a
        ld a,l
        inc a
        and $0f
        ld l,a
        ld (Ptr),hl
        pop hl
        pop af
        ei
        reti
Ptr:    .defw Table
        .org $9000
Table:  .defb 128,177,218,245,255,245,218,177,128,79,38,11,1,11,38,79
```

Channel 0's interrupt comes through vector `$A6`, so the word at `$BEA6` holds the address of `Isr`. Now the
rate is 28,000,000 ÷ 16 ÷ 109 = 16,055 interrupts a second, the same **1,003 Hz**, and it stays 1,003 Hz at
every CPU speed, because the CTC never looks at the CPU's clock. The CPU still writes every sample, but only
for the interrupt routine, about 150 T-states with the interrupt itself; between samples it is free to run your game.

**The DMA.** The Next's DMA can copy bytes from memory to a port by itself, and its **prescaler** paces them:
one byte every 32 × P master clocks, so about **875,000 ÷ P** bytes a second. Set it up once, point it at the
sample and the DAC's port, and it plays the whole thing while the CPU does something else:

```z80klive
        .org $8000
Start:  di
        nextreg $07,0
        nextreg $08,$58
        ld hl,DmaProg
        ld b,16           ; the 16 bytes from DmaProg
Send:   ld a,(hl)
        out ($6b),a       ; the zxnDMA's port
        inc hl
        djnz Send
Park:   jr Park
DmaProg:
        .defb $83                 ; disable
        .defb $7d,$00,$c0,16,0    ; WR0: A to B, from $C000, 16 bytes
        .defb $14                 ; WR1: port A is memory, incrementing
        .defb $68,$21,55         ; WR2: port B is I/O, fixed; prescaler 55
        .defb $cd,$df,$00         ; WR4: burst mode, port B = $00DF
        .defb $a2                 ; WR5: auto restart
        .defb $cf,$87             ; load, enable
        .org $c000
        .defb 128,177,218,245,255,245,218,177,128,79,38,11,1,11,38,79
```

Two settings matter here. **Burst mode** makes the DMA give the bus back to the CPU while it waits for the next
byte; in continuous mode it would keep the bus, and with it the CPU, for the whole block. And **auto restart**
starts the block again at its end, so these 16 bytes play for ever. With P = 55, the prescaler alone would give
875,000 ÷ 55 = 15,909 bytes a second, 994 Hz. You get a little less, **968 Hz** at 3.5 MHz, because each byte
also costs a few CPU clocks to take the bus back from the CPU. But the rate barely moves with the CPU's speed:
964 Hz at 7 MHz.

**The Copper.** The Copper can write NextRegs, and NextReg `$2D` writes channels A and D. So a Copper list that
waits for each line and then moves the next sample into `$2D` plays a sample with no CPU at all, at one sample
a line: 15,625 a second at 48K timing, 15,351 at 128K timing. Its limits are just as clear. The samples have to
sit in the list itself, which holds 1,024 instructions, and to play every frame the list starts again at
each frame (the Copper's mode `11`), so its samples repeat with the frame. Fine for a
short looping sound; a long recording needs the CPU to keep rewriting the list.

Here are the three captured, each at two CPU speeds:

{/* /fig:speeds */}
<figcaption>The same 16-step sine table played three ways, each at two CPU speeds. The CPU loop doubles its pitch at 7 MHz; the DMA and the CTC keep theirs.</figcaption>
</figure>

So which one should you use? Here is my advice. For a sample that plays alongside a game, use the **DMA** in
burst mode: one setup, no CPU, and a rate that does not care how fast the CPU runs. For effects that have to be
computed sample by sample, mixing two sounds, say, use the **CTC** and do the work in the interrupt routine.
Keep the **CPU loop** for the moments when nothing else runs anyway, and for software that brings its own. And the **Copper** is a lovely trick for a short sound in a program whose Copper has a few
instructions to spare.

## A drum hit

Time for something that no generator can play. These are 2,048 bytes of noise that die away, a rough stand-in for a
snare drum, played once by the DMA at prescaler 55:

{/* /fig:drum */}
<figcaption>A drum hit: 2,048 samples played once by the DMA in burst mode, prescaler 55, to port <code>$DF</code>. Without auto restart, the DMA stops at the last byte, and the channel holds that byte.</figcaption>
</figure>

The block lasts 2,048 × 32 × 55 ÷ 28,000,000 = 128.7 ms by the prescaler alone, and a little longer with each
byte's few extra clocks, about 131 ms. Notice the end. Without auto restart the DMA stops after the last byte,
and the channel does what every DAC channel does: it **holds** that byte. A sample should therefore end at
`$80`, or near it; one that ends anywhere else leaves its last level behind. That is also why a drum over AY
music costs so little: start the DMA, go back to the game, and the drum takes care of itself.

## The DACs in the mixer

Each side's two channels add up to 0–510, and the mixer counts that four times, so the DACs take **0 to 2,040
units** of the mixer's 0–8,191 per side:

<figure>
{/* fig:budget */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 230" role="img" aria-label="Two bars drawn to scale on the mixer's range of 0 to 8,191 for one side. The DACs' range on a side is 0 to 2,040 units, a quarter of the whole. At rest, both channels of the side at $80, they add 1,024 units, the middle of their range; a sample swings from there down to 0 and up to 2,040." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>
  <text x="0" y="22" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">the DACs' range on one side: two channels × 255 × 4</text>
  <rect x="0" y="32" width="698" height="30" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1.5" strokeOpacity="0.3" />
  <rect x="0" y="32" width="173.8" height="30" fill="#30a46c" fillOpacity="0.7" stroke="#30a46c" strokeWidth="1" />
  <text x="86.9" y="53" textAnchor="middle" fill="#ffffff" fontSize="13.5" fontWeight="bold">0–2,040</text>
  <text x="0" y="90" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">at rest: both channels at $80</text>
  <rect x="0" y="100" width="698" height="30" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1.5" strokeOpacity="0.3" />

  <line x1="87.3" y1="130" x2="87.3" y2="158" stroke="#30a46c" strokeWidth="2" strokeDasharray="5 4" />
  <text x="95.3" y="156" fill="#30a46c" fontSize="13.5">a sample swings about here</text>

{/* /fig:budget */}
<figcaption>The DACs' share of one side of the mixer, to scale. At rest, both channels at <code>$80</code>, they add 1,024 units, the middle of their range.</figcaption>
</figure>

At rest the DACs are not silent in the mixer's eyes: two channels at `$80` add 1,024 units a side, and that
is most of the 1,536 a silent Next sits at (the Pi adds the other 512). A full-scale sample on both channels of a
side swings from there down to 0 and up to 2,040, four times the beeper's 512; through a mono port, one channel
a side, it swings about 1,020, twice the beeper. The mixer
chapter's [Headroom](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#headroom) shows what happens when
the DACs, the AY chips and the beeper all shout at once.

## How Klive does it

In Klive, the DACs are one small module of the Next core, a C program compiled to WebAssembly. It keeps the
four channel bytes and NextReg `$08` bit 3; while the bit is clear it holds every channel at `$80` and ignores
every write. A port write takes the port's low byte, looks at the NextReg `$84` bits of the cards that own that
byte, and writes the channels the map gives, with Soundrive mode 2's rule for `$FB`. NextRegs `$2C`, `$2D` and
`$2E` write B, A and D, and C. The port decoding also keeps writes to `$F1` and `$F9` away from the paging
ports while Soundrive mode 2 is on.

The timers are the Next's own. Klive's DMA counts its prescaler in master clocks, whatever the CPU's speed, and
hands the bus back and forth in burst mode as the hardware does, which is where the few extra clocks a byte come
from. Its CTC counts the 28 MHz clock through the prescaler and the time constant, and its Copper writes NextReg
`$2D` like any other.

When the mixer needs a sample, Klive adds each side's two channels and, as for every source, takes them about
their rest level: a side at rest adds 0 to Klive's mix rather than the hardware's 1,024. The sound you hear is the
same; only the number that stands for silence differs, as the mixer chapter's
[How Klive does it](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#how-klive-does-it) explains. And if
you want to read the code yourself, here is where it lives:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The four channels, the port map, the enables and the NextReg mirrors | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-dac.c` |
| Port decoding, with the `$F1`/`$F9` rule for the paging ports | `zxnext-ports.c` in the same folder |
| The DMA and its prescaler | `zxnext-dma.c` in the same folder |
| The CTC | `zxnext-ctc.c` in the same folder |
| The DACs' sides in the sum | `zxnext-audio-mixer.c` in the same folder |

## What to take home

A DAC channel is a number that holds. You write `$00` to `$FF`, the level jumps there and stays until the next
write, and `$80` is silence. The Next has four channels, **A and B on the left, C and D on the right**; each side
adds its two, 0 to 510, and the mixer counts them four times, up to 2,040 units. Bit 3 of NextReg `$08` switches
them on; it is off after power-on, and while it is off every channel holds `$80`.

The Next answers on the ports of six cards: Soundrive in its two modes, Profi Covox, Covox, Pentagon and ATM, GS
Covox and Specdrum, each enabled by one bit, 1 to 7, of NextReg `$84`. Only a port's low byte counts. Mono ports
write one channel on each side; `$FB` is Soundrive mode 2's channel D when that mode is on and Pentagon's A and D
when it is off; and NextRegs `$2C`, `$2D` and `$2E` write B, A and D, and C from code or the Copper.

What decides how a sample sounds is who times the writes. A **CPU loop** plays at the CPU's speed and takes all
of the CPU. The **CTC** interrupts at 28 MHz ÷ 16 or 256 ÷ its time constant, the **DMA** moves a byte every
32 × prescaler master clocks, a little under 875,000 ÷ P a second, and the **Copper** can write one sample a
line. Those three keep their rate at any CPU speed, and the DMA in burst mode leaves the CPU almost all its
time. 24:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["A DAC channel is an ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"8-bit register"}]," with a converter behind it. Your program writes a value, ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$00"}]," to\n",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FF"}],", and the channel turns it into a level. 3c:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Here comes the part that decides whether a sample sounds right. A DAC plays a sample at the rate your writes\narrive, and only at that rate. Write the steps of a recording made at 16,000 samples a second 16,000 times a\nsecond, and it sounds as it was recorded. Write them faster and the voice turns into a chipmunk; write them at\nan uneven pace and the sound gets a rough, gritty edge. So the real question about a DAC is not how to write it\n(any ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"out"}]," will do) but ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"who decides when"}],". The Next gives you four answers:"]}]
3d:["$","figure",null,{"children":[["$","svg",null,{"viewBox":"0 0 700 360","role":"img","aria-label":"A diagram of four ways to time DAC writes. The CPU clock, 3.5, 7, 14 or 28 MHz as NextReg $07 sets it, drives a CPU loop: its rate follows the CPU's speed. The 28 MHz master clock drives the other three: the CTC, which divides it by 16 or 256 and then by its time constant and interrupts the CPU, whose interrupt routine writes the sample; the DMA, which moves one byte every 32 times its prescaler master clocks; and the Copper, which can write a DAC through its NextReg mirror once per line. 47:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Two settings matter here. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Burst mode"}]," makes the DMA give the bus back to the CPU while it waits for the next\nbyte; in continuous mode it would keep the bus, and with it the CPU, for the whole block. And ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"auto restart"}],"\nstarts the block again at its end, so these 16 bytes play for ever. With P = 55, the prescaler alone would give\n875,000 ÷ 55 = 15,909 bytes a second, 994 Hz. You get a little less, ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"968 Hz"}]," at 3.5 MHz, because each byte\nalso costs a few CPU clocks to take the bus back from the CPU. But the rate barely moves with the CPU’s speed:\n964 Hz at 7 MHz."]}]
48:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The Copper."}]," The Copper can write NextRegs, and NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$2D"}]," writes channels A and D. Its limits are just as clear. The samples have to\nsit in the list itself, which holds 1,024 instructions, and to play every frame the list starts again at\neach frame (the Copper’s mode ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"11"}],"), so its samples repeat with the frame. Fine for a\nshort looping sound; a long recording needs the CPU to keep rewriting the list."]}]

For a sample that plays alongside a game, use the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"DMA"}]," in\nburst mode: one setup, no CPU, and a rate that does not care how fast the CPU runs.
